Challenge these proposals under ONLY the supplied task rubric. Evaluate each criterion pass/fail/unknown with score 0–5 and a concrete rationale. Use unknown for evidence gaps, fail for established constraint violations. Entailment concerns reported facts, not proof of an explicitly untested prediction. An unmeasured bottleneck can be the first falsification question; do not demand a proven speedup before proposing a test. No-GPU-execution constraints prohibit launching jobs in this research session, not describing a later bounded experiment. A full-text source existing does not mean the quoted passage supports the claim. No search hits is uncertainty. Novelty is irrelevant unless explicitly enabled by the rubric. Score 0–5 with explicit objections. High polish must not rescue a failed gate. Known techniques, feature activation and ports are legitimate candidates if useful relative to the baseline. An explicit cheap baseline/eligibility check can resolve unknown deployment inclusion before implementation. Do not repeat historical novelty rejection as a current veto. Review each ID exactly once.

This PR sets those slots to 0 and instead caps running requests (\nmax_num_active_seqs\n) at\nmax_num_batched_tokens // num_lookahead_tokens\n, so the separate draft pass still fits its buffers.\nFixes\n#52922\n.\nClaims\nDFlash/DSpark requests no longer pay for draft slots. A DSpark decode with K = 5 is charged 6 tokens instead of 10. With\n--max-num-batched-tokens 512\nthat raises running requests from 52 to 64 and output throughput from 1373 to 3390 tok/s (DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash + DSpark on GB300, table below).\nThe draft pass stays within its\nmax_num_batched_tokens\n-row buffers. A request cap does this instead of the slots, and it only binds when\nmax_num_seqs \u00d7 num_lookahead_tokens > max_num_batched_tokens\n.\nThis applies to DFlash on both model runners and to DSpark (MRV2-only), through the shared scheduler config. When any rank has prefill, the step becomes a long mixed (and, on some backends, eager) step for every rank. Agentic multi-turn traffic makes prefill frequent, so decode keeps getting interrupted.\n--dp-prefill-delayer\n(opt-in, default off) aligns prefill across the ranks and protects the decode steps after a prefill. It follows ATOM's PrefillDelayer.\nClaims\nMeasured on a development branch with an earlier version of this change: DeepSeek-V4-Pro on 8x MI355X, DP8 attention + EP8, AgentX agentic workload, 1200 s runs, metrics over 300-1200 s, single runs per configuration.\nConcurrency\nConfiguration\nP90 interactivity (tok/s/user)\nP50 interactivity (tok/s/user)\nTotal tok/s/GPU\nTTFT p50 (s)\nNode\n64\nno delayer (\n--prefill-schedule-interval 32\n), mean of 2 runs\n16.65\n31.03\n22.0K\n3.76\nA, B\n64\n+ prefill delayer (10 protected steps)\n45.54\n56.89\n22.2K\n8.63\nA\n128\nno delayer (\n--prefill-schedule-interval 32\n)\n7.29\n11.96\n23.8K\n7.47\nD\n128\nprefill delayer, 10 protected steps\n22.82\n26.97\n39.7K\n5.41\nC\n128\nprefill delayer, 20 protected steps\n26.36\n28.92\n38.6K\n10.03\nC\nThe c128 rows use a later recipe with other changes; they differ only in the prefill policy. The no-delayer c128 row ran on a different node of the same type.\nThe trade-off is TTFT: prefill waits for the other ranks, and long prompts wait for alignment chunk by chunk.\nValidation on this upstream branch is still to be added.\nValidation\npytest tests/v1/core/test_prefill_delayer.py\n: 14 passed (CPU). The tests cover each FIRE / HOLD rule and bound, the per-rank state, the scheduler gating and the config check.\npytest -m cpu_test tests/v1/core/test_scheduler.py tests/v1/core/test_async_scheduler.py\nand\ntests/engine/test_arg_utils.py\n: pass. They cover the changed\ndefer_prefills\nexpression with the delayer off.\npre-commit run --files <changed files>\n: all hooks pass, mypy included.\nNo GPU runs on this branch yet.\nDetails\nProtocol.\nOnce per engine step, each rank reports 6 ints in one CPU SUM all-reduce on the DP group: prefillable, pending prefill tokens (capped at\nmax_num_batched_tokens\n), running decodes, KV usage >= 0.9, chunk in progress, prefill ran. Every rank then takes the same FIRE / HOLD decision for its next schedule.\nPlacement.\nThe all-reduce sits next to\n_has_global_unfinished_reqs()\n. Every rank reaches that point on every non-idle step (real, dummy and pause steps alike), which the existing DP state sync already relies on, so the extra collective cannot be skipped by one rank. The state resets at each DP wave boundary.\nRules (ATOM's), in order:\nA step that ran prefill arms\n--dp-prefill-delayer-protected-steps\n(default 10) steps that HOLD while decodes run.\nFIRE if no rank has prefill, no rank decodes, or KV usage is high.\nFIRE after 200 held steps, or after 100 with a chunk in progress.\nHOLD until all ranks have prefill.\nFIRE when the pending tokens reach 0.9 x prefillable ranks x\nmax_num_batched_tokens\n, or when the backlog stops growing for 10 steps.\nHOLD scope.\nHOLD defers all prefill compute, new requests and in-progress chunks, even on a rank without local decodes, so that ranks prefill together.\nInteraction with\n--prefill-schedule-interval\n.\nThe delayer replaces it; setting both is an error.\nCost.\nOff: none. On: one 6-int CPU all-reduce per step.\nNot supported:\nelastic EP and custom scheduler classes.\nRelation to\n[Perf] Add an adaptive prefill delayer for data-parallel serving\n#51199\n(cc\n@kjiang249\n). That PR coordinates releases through the DP coordinator (asynchronous, with acks and a 2-step lead); this one uses a synchronous per-step all-reduce in the engine loop and adds protected decode steps and a token fill target. We drove both decision functions in a CPU simulation of 8 lockstep ranks with agentic traffic:\n[Perf] Add an adaptive prefill delayer for data-parallel serving\n#51199\n's all-ranks release rarely triggered; most of its releases came from its capacity and 30-pass fail-open bounds.\nThis delayer had 2-4x fewer mixed steps and about half the mean decode latency.\n[Perf] Add an adaptive prefill delayer for data-parallel serving\n#51199\nhad about 3x lower TTFT.\nThe two are complementary, and I am happy to merge efforts.\nAI assistance (Claude Code) was used for this PR.\nPull Request Checklist\nI used vLLM's\n/pr-checklist\nskill. Markdown\nContributor\nPurpose\nDeepSeek-V4.1's Engram tables are already MXFP8 (FP8 rows with one ue8m0 scale per 32 values) and\nwkv\nis an MXFP8 GEMM, but the lookup dequantizes each row to BF16, DP replicas all-gather those 512-byte rows layer by layer, and\nwkv\nquantizes them right back. This PR keeps rows packed from the host table to\nwkv\n.\nPacked rows, direct MXFP8.\nThe lookup copies each row's 256 FP8 bytes and 8 scale bytes as they are, and one unpack kernel writes them in FlashInfer's MXFP8 activation layout (F8_128x4 scales).\nwkv\ntakes them as a\nQuantizedActivation\n, so both the dequant and the requant are gone, and the output is bit-identical.\nOne all-to-all per prefill step.\nEngramBatch\nlooks up every local Engram layer into one packed buffer and exchanges it with a single all-to-all, which sends each rank only its own tokens (264 instead of 512 bytes per head row, 1/DP of an all-gather), then unpacks all layers in one launch.\nPeer exchange for decode.\nBelow 128 tokens per rank,\nEngramPeerExchange\nreplaces the hash all-gather and the per-layer row all-gathers: ranks publish ids in symmetric memory, look up their head shard for every peer's tokens, and read their own tokens back from the peers after a barrier. All layers share one lookup and one unpack launch.\nThe batched path serves DP-sharded tables (\nembedding_across_dp\nwithout DP-shared memory) with TP = 1,\nhead_dim % 128 == 0\n, no LoRA (it wraps\nwkv\nafter construction), and a\nwkv\nkernel that takes F8_128x4 scales (reusing\ncan_fuse_query_quant\n). The peer exchange also needs SM100, CPU offload, and 2/4/8 fully connected GPUs; without it every slot uses the all-to-all, which on DEP4 HBM tables at 1\u2013127 tokens per rank is 19\u201358 us faster than the BF16 path. while loading.\nPlease reload this page\n.\nCopy link\nCopy Markdown\nContributor\nOverview\nWith\ndp_shared_memory\n, node-local DP ranks share one registered\n/dev/shm\nmapping for the Engram host tables (2 layers x 94.4 GiB on DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash). Lookups are random row reads and on GB200 they are bound by GPU TLB misses: the registered mapping uses 64K pages.\nThis PR allocates the shared table as host-NUMA cuMem instead, which the GPU maps with 2M pages. Rank 0 creates it and broadcasts a FABRIC handle; the other node-local DP ranks import and map it. Where FABRIC handles are unavailable (probed once: create + export), the existing\n/dev/shm\npath is used and logged. Rank 0 decides the backing and broadcasts it so every rank runs the same collectives. the diff shows only this PR's three commits). DeepSeek-V4.1's layers past the last KV-source layer (21-39 on V4.1-Flash) own nothing but sliding-window KV: every later layer attends the compressed KV of the source layers, and only the last 128 tokens' window KV is read after prefill. This is DeepSeek's decoder-side SWA bounded replay: in prefill those layers run on each request's last 128 tokens only, cold prefills included, while layers 0-20 still run on every token. It is a deliberate approximation, not an equivalent reduction of work: the kept rows' window attention in layers 21-39 is bounded at the trailing window (the SWA kernels read no window KV below it), so the last token's hidden state is not bit-identical to a full prefill; the long-range context reaches it through the compressed KV. The accuracy runs below (gsm8k on TP4 and DP4, two passes each) and DSpark's unchanged acceptance length are what this rests on.\nDeepseekV4Model\n(\nnvidia/model.py\n): when the layers after the last KV source are all sliding-window and the configuration allows it, the forward is split into\n_run_layers\nup to the cut,\nDecoderReplayLayers\nfor the rest, and the final mHC collapse. Otherwise the forward is unchanged and a warning names the reason.\nDeepseekV41ModelState.prepare_attn\n(\nnvidia/model_state.py\n): after the step's own metadata it builds the replay layers' batch from the same inputs: the replay rows (one Triton kernel fills the rows, the row-subset\nInputBuffers\n, its slot mappings and its replay start), attention metadata through metadata builders of its own (the runner's hold the batch's metadata past the forward) and, under piecewise graphs, the size of the replay layers' graph. Everything lives in persistent buffers because that graph reads them by address. The result is set on\nDecoderReplayLayers.replay_batch\n; it is None when nothing trims in an eager forward and under FULL graphs (uniform decode).\nDecoderReplayLayers\n(\ndecoder_replay_layers.py\n): gathers the seven layer inputs by the rows, runs the layers under a copy of the forward context with the replay batch's attention metadata, slot mappings, padding mask, batch descriptor and DP metadata, and scatters the outputs back to full-batch rows. Under breakable piecewise graphs the replay is an eager break of the model graph: the layers run in a graph of their own (\nReplayCudaGraphs\n, keyed by the replay batch's padded size) captured inside the model graph's capture of the same size. Only prefills trim, so a capture's dummy batch keeps all its rows and every capture size gets a replay graph of that size; at runtime the replay batch pads to the smallest capture size that fits, which therefore always exists.\nData parallelism: the replay layers' MoE collectives need every rank's token count, so the model state has the ranks agree in\nprepare_attn\nwith one CPU all-reduce carrying whether each rank trims and its replay token count. Any rank trimming makes every rank replay on those counts; under graphs all ranks run the one replay graph size that fits the largest count.\nDSpark (\ndspark/speculator.py\n): the drafter derives its context KV from the target's aux hidden states, which under decoder replay exist only for the last window of each request;\n_context_rows\nbounds the context KV writes to the drafter's own window.\nShared changes, none behavioral for other models:\nAttentionGroup.make_metadata_builder\n(a builder outside the group's own list),\nprepare_inputs_to_capture\nhands the model state the capture's real cudagraph mode (the default state treats PIECEWISE like NONE), and an eager break of a breakable capture may capture graphs of its own.\nThe decoder side stays off, with a warning, when the pipeline stage does not hold every layer after the last KV source, under sequence-parallel MoE, prefill context parallelism or microbatching, with an Engram layer after the cut, or with a drafter whose window is wider than the target's. DeepSeek-V4.1 turns sequence-parallel MoE on for EP with TP > 1 under DP, so under DP the decoder side runs with TP = 1 today.\nNo other open PR implements the decoder side of SWA replay;\n#56227\nis the encoder side this builds on.\nUsage\nNothing to configure: with\n#56227\nand this PR, DeepSeek-V4.1 on model runner V2 replays on both sides by default, including under\n--data-parallel-size > 1\nwith TP = 1.\n--no-swa-bounded-replay\nturns both sides off.\nTest Plan\npytest tests/models/test_deepseek_v41_replay_batch.py tests/models/test_deepseek_v4_decoder_replay_layers.py \\\n 3618361f2a66..010d517d4645 100644\n--- a/vllm/distributed/utils.py\n+++ b/vllm/distributed/utils.py\n@@ -55,6 +55,70 @@ def ensure_divisibility(numerator, denominator):\n     )\n \n \n+# P2P tags [base, base + log2(group size) + 1] are reserved for cpu_all_reduce_.\n+_CPU_ALL_REDUCE_TAG_BASE = 27100\n+\n+\n+def cpu_all_reduce_(tensor: torch.Tensor, group: ProcessGroup) -> torch.Tensor:\n+    \"\"\"Low-latency in-place SUM all-reduce of a small CPU tensor over a gloo group.\n+\n+    Recursive doubling within power-of-two blocks, then one concurrent exchange\n+    between blocks. Results are bitwise identical across ranks. Works with\n+    stateless (unregistered) process groups.\n+    \"\"\"\n+    world_size = group.size()\n+    if world_size == 1:\n+        return tensor\n+    block_size = world_size & -world_size\n+    num_blocks = world_size // block_size\n+    if tensor.device.type != \"cpu\" or not tensor.is_contiguous():\n+        torch.distributed.all_reduce(tensor, group=group)\n+        return tensor\n+\n+    rank = group.rank()\n+    tag = _CPU_ALL_REDUCE_TAG_BASE\n+\n+    # Recursive doubling within the block (peers stay in the aligned block).\n+    if block_size > 1:\n+        # This branch applies only to even world sizes.\n+        scratch = torch.empty_like(tensor)\n+        mask = 1\n+        while mask < block_size:\n+            peer = rank ^ mask\n+            # Wait for both ops before touching either buffer.\n+            recv_work = group.recv([scratch], peer, tag)\n+            send_work = group.send([tensor], peer, tag)\n+            recv_work.wait()\n+            send_work.wait()\n+            tensor.add_(scratch)\n+            mask <<= 1\n+            tag += 1\n+\n+    # Exchange block sums with the same lane in every other block at once.\n+    if num_blocks > 1:\n+        # This branch applies only when world_size is not a power of two.\n+        lane, my_block = rank % block_size, rank // block_size\n+        block_sums = [\n+            tensor if b == my_block else torch.empty_like(tensor)\n+            for b in range(num_blocks)\n+        ]\n+        works = []\n+        for b, buf in enumerate(block_sums):\n+            if b != my_block:\n+                works.append(group.recv([buf], b * block_size + lane, tag))\n+        for b in range(num_blocks):\n+            if b != my_block:\n+                works.append(group.send([tensor], b * block_size + lane, tag))\n+        for work in works:\n+            work.wait()\n+        # Sum in block order so every rank computes the identical result.\n+        total = block_sums[0]\n+        for buf in block_sums[1:]:\n+            total.add_(buf)\n+        tensor.copy_(total)\n+    return tensor\n+\n+\n def divide(numerator, denominator):\n     \"\"\"Ensure that numerator is divisible by the denominator and return\n     the division value.\"\"\"\n\ndiff --git a/vllm/v1/worker/gpu/dp_utils.py b/vllm/v1/worker/gpu/dp_utils.py\nindex 5c71ebcea018..2bcad34e0560 100644\n--- a/vllm/v1/worker/gpu/dp_utils.py\n+++ b/vllm/v1/worker/gpu/dp_utils.py\n@@ -5,7 +5,6 @@\n from dataclasses import dataclass, replace\n \n import torch\n-import tor", "body_excerpted": true, "metadata_excerpt": "{\"sha256\": \"dbfc95a00d977155b275e983e347b269df832e4a9eb924c01b071b28d74a9438\", \"patch_commit_shas\": [\"7b544ecb5bdd4f0bd61689e184158611c692fcaf\", \"c5b750ffaa69a70cd6d2992b6ccfcebf0494a9bb\"], \"snapshot\": \"/home/ubuntu/AutoResearch-Inference/ideation/runs/h200-fresh-research-001/primary/vllm-project--vllm--61767/changes.patch\"}", "metadata_excerpted": false}, {"id": "github:deepseek-ai/DeepEP:693:live-page", "source": "github", "kind": "resource", "url": "https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepEP/pull/693", "title": "Pipeline direct NVLink dispatch with triple-buffered prefetch by usernamehaha2022 \u00b7 Pull Request #693 \u00b7 deepseek-ai/DeepEP \u00b7 GitHub", "created_at": null, "updated_at": null, "fetched_at": "2026-10-20T12:15:56Z", "state": null, "merged_at": null, "parent": "github:deepseek-ai/DeepEP:693", "content_hash": "5ec321e2d8b15d15527184de4a26effa2209c56e52aad0342ffac64278361bc6", "body": "Conversation\nusernamehaha2022\ncommented\nJul 22, 2026\n\u2022\nedited\nLoading\nUh oh!\nThere was an error while loading.\nPlease reload this page\n.\nCopy link\nCopy Markdown\nSummary\nThis PR pipelines direct NVLink dispatch using three shared-memory TMA buffers per dispatch warp.\nPreviously, a dispatch warp prepared tokens sequentially around remote S2G stores. With this change, the next token's hidden data, scale factors, metadata, and routing information are prefetched while previous remote stores remain in flight.\nAt steady state, the three buffers are used for:\nA previous token whose remote S2G store may still be in flight.\nThe current token being sent to remote ranks.\nThe next token being prefetched from global memory.\nBefore recycling the oldest buffer,\ntma_store_wait<1>()\nreduces the number of outstanding store groups to one. This keeps buffer reuse safe without fully draining all remote stores between tokens.\nThe shared-memory calculation now accounts for all three staging buffers, and direct NVLink dispatch is limited to at most 14 dispatch warps. The non-NVLink path remains single-buffered.\nValidation\nTested on 8 x NVIDIA H100 GPUs with direct NVLink dispatch.\nCUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7 \\\nEP_DISABLE_GIN=1 \\\npython -u tests/elastic/test_ep.py \\\n  --num-processes 8 \\\n  --allow-hybrid-mode 0 \\\n  --test-first-only \\\n  --ignore-local-traffic\nAverage across 8 ranks:\nMetric\nmain\nThis PR\nChange\nDispatch\n250GB/s\n280 GB/s\n30\nCombine\n300 GB/s\n300 GB/s\n-\nWith rank unbalanced ratio set to 1.5:\nMetric\nmain\nThis PR\nChange\nDispatch\n203GB/s\n220 GB/s\n17\nCombine\n209 GB/s\n209 GB/s\n-\nSorry, something went wrong.\nUh oh!\nThere was an error while loading.\nPlease reload this page\n.\nAll reactions\n\u7384\u6b66\nadded\n2\ncommits\nJuly 22, 2026 12:58\nperf: double-buffer direct NVLink dispatch\nb9ef9b3\nperf: prefetch direct dispatch tokens across NVLink stores\nd2be99a\nds-review-bot\nreviewed\nJul 22, 2026\nView reviewed changes\nComment thread\ndeep_ep/include/deep_ep/impls/dispatch.cuh\nOutdated\nComment on lines\n+264\nto\n+277\nconstexpr int kNumTmaBuffers = kIsScaleupNVLink ? implementations focus on generating a single sequence.\nReal-world generative AI applications often require multiple responses and how to perform speculative decoding in a batched setting while preserving its latency benefits poses non-trivial challenges.\nThis paper describes a system of batched speculati\n[... excerpt gap ...]\n\n7 Limitations\n\n\n\n\nThis work, while advancing the state of the art, does not solve the efficient inference challenge of LLMs.\nFor example, GPU utilization during the context encoding phase of LLM inference can be over 70% in our system, while the best achievable utilization during the incremental decoding phase is 15.8% in this paper.\nAlthough this is already significantly better than previous works, there is clearly substantial room to innovate and improve.\n\n\n\n\n8 Ethical Impact\n\n\n\n\nThis work aims to increase the efficiency of deploying LLMs by utilizing the compute resources efficiently.\nIt can reduce carbon emissions associated with LLM deployment.\nAdditionally, driving down infrastructure costs can potentially encourage broader LLM adoption.\nImpact of increased LLM usage on the society and associated risks are difficult to forecast.\n\n\n\n\nReferences\n\n\n \n\nAminabadi et\u00a0al. Cache-blind balancing disperses that reuse; fixed affinity preserves it but can overload a server. CacheRoute resolves this tradeoff with a periodic routing plan. It admits high-rate keys to a stable warm set and places their assignments by expected load. Hot keys may use more than one destination, although every key in our primary semi-synthetic \n[... excerpt gap ...]\n\n8 Conclusion\n\n\n\n\nCacheRoute plans prefix affinity and expected load once per control interval. On a 70B fp8 model across 60 H100 GPUs, it reaches 93.2% served KV hit and 2.3\u00d72.3\\times the capacity of the strongest baseline at a 3.5-s p99 SLO. The negative workloads set an equally important boundary: affinity can reduce capacity when the recoverable prefix work is small. Any real deployment would therefore measure its own boundary with shadow replay.\n\n\n\n\n\n\nSupplementary Material\n\n\n\n\nThe supplement records experiment provenance, secondary runs, and negative results. It adds no headline claims. Each result is labeled as multi-run hardware, single-window hardware, or calibrated simulation.\n\n\n\n\nAppendix A Workload Detail\n\n\n\nA.1 Semi-synthetic aggregate statistics\n\n\n\n\nThe routing key is an opaque business identifier, and the planner reads aggregate counts rather than request text. Table\u00a06 reports de-identified summaries derived from operational serving telemetry and distinguishes overall prompt structure from the segment whose residency the router can affect.\n\n\nTable 6: Primary workload characteristics. \u201cPositional prefix\u201d is not the same as the business-specific routing lever.\n\n\n\nProperty\nMeasured value\n\nDistinct business keys\n128,824\n[... excerpt gap ...]\n\n Experimental support, please\n view the build logs\n for errors. Copyright contributors to the vLLM project\n\"\"\"DSpark speculator: semi-autoregressive parallel drafting.\n\nDSpark drafts a block of ``num_speculative_tokens`` tokens in one parallel pass\n(reusing Anchor-as-first-prediction: each request emits exactly ``N =\n    num_speculative_tokens`` query tokens (anchor + N-1 noise), NOT ``1 + N``.\n    Every query position is a prediction (the anchor predicts the first draft\n    token), so we sample at all N positions and ``sample_pos = query_pos + 1``\n    (standard next-token), whereas DFlash's masks sit AT the predicted position.\n    This is the ``sample_from_anchor`` path in the shared prepare-inputs kernel.\n    Speculators-format checkpoints instead use the DFlash ``1 + N`` fill-in\n    layout (anchor is the bonus token).\n  * Sequential Markov sampling: instead of DFlash's single parallel sample, we\n    sample left-to-right, adding a prefix-dependent Markov bias derived from the\n    previously sampled token at each step.\n\nCUDA graphs (FULL, mirroring DFlash) cover the whole draft step: the parallel\nbackbone forward AND the sequential Markov sampling.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom typing import Any\n\nimport torch\n\nfrom coverage for [XiaomiMiMo/MiMo-V2.6-Flash-RL](https://huggingface.co/XiaomiMiMo/MiMo-V2.6-Flash-RL), a 309B-total / 15B-active MiMoV2 model, on B200 with upstream SGLang and upstream vLLM. Neither key uses speculative decoding yet.\n\n**What runs:** two new keys, 24 points in total. Every point keeps the KV cache on the GPU (no CPU offload).\n\n| Key | Image | Points |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `mimov2.6flash-fp4-b200-sglang-agentic` | `lmsysorg/sglang:v0.5.21-cu130` | TP4 at conc 1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64; TP2 at 1, 4, 8, 16, 32 |\n| `mimov2.6flash-fp4-b200-vllm-agentic` | `vllm/vllm-openai:v0.31.0` | TP8 at conc 1, 4; TP4 at 1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64; TP2 at 1, 4, 8, 16, 32 |\n\nBoth images are pinned by digest. Admission is 2\u00d7 concurrency for subagent fan-out, and decode CUDA graphs cover the concurrency.\n\n**Precision (`fp4`), checked against the checkpoint's safetensors headers:**\n- Routed experts, about 98% of the parameters, are packed MXFP4 (E2M1 with an E8M0 scale per 32 values).\n- Dense MLP, fused QKV and MTP layers are FP8 E4M3 with 128\u00d7128 block scales.\n- `o_proj`, router, embeddings and `lm_head` are BF16.\n- On B200 both engines run the routed experts natively as MXFP4 weights \u00d7 MXFP8 activations (FlashInfer TRT-LLM), and the FP8 layers as block-scaled FP8. This is the same mixed layout as `deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash`, which this repo labels `fp4`.\n\n**SGLang settings** (from the [SGLang MiMo-V2.5 cookbook](https://docs.sglang.io/cookbook/autoregressive/Xiaomi/MiMo-V2.5); V2.6-Flash uses the same `MiMoV2ForCausalLM` architecture and size class):\n- `moe-runner-backend: flashinfer_mxfp4`. v0.5.21's MiMo default picks the FP8 TRT-LLM runner on SM100 for any `quant_method: fp8` checkpoint, which cannot read packed MXFP4 experts. The upstream fix (sgl-project/sglang#41668) landed after v0.5.21.\n- `attention-backend: fa4`, because the default `trtllm_mha` rejects MiMoV2's asymmetric K/V head dims on Blackwell.\n- No `language-model-only`: v0.5.21 rejects it for `MiMoV2ForCausalLM` (the first canary failed at startup on it). The vision and audio encoders (~2 GB) therefore load, with `mm-attention-backend: fa4` for the ViT; text-only AgentX requests never run them.\n- `chunked-prefill-size 16384`, `mem-fraction-static 0.85`, `mimo` reasoning and tool-call parsers.\n- No TP8: the fused QKV weights are interleaved for 4-way TP (`num_key_value_heads = 4`), and SGLang requires the attention TP size to divide 4.\n\n**vLLM settings** (from [vLLM's MiMo-V2.6-Flash-RL recipe](https://github.com/vllm-project/recipes/blob/main/models/XiaomiMiMo/MiMo-V2.6-Flash-RL.yaml), which names v0.31.0 as the first release that loads this checkpoint):\n- `gpu-memory-utilization 0.95`, `max-model-len auto` (the largest context the KV pool holds, for the uncapped AgentX corpus), `max-num-batched-tokens 16384`, `mimo` parsers.\n- TP8 is valid here: vLLM regroups the fused QKV per rank and replicates KV heads.\n- vLLM's `MiMoV2ForCausalLM` is text-only, so no `language-model-only` flag is needed.\n- KV cache stays BF16: the MiMo FP8 KV fix (vllm-project/vllm#58128) landed after v0.31.0.\n\nThinking stays on for both engines, which is the chat template's default.\n\n**Known gaps:**\n- **No speculative decoding yet.** `infx/golden_al_distribution/` has no MiMo curve, so a speculative run would fail at launch. #4331 adds a vLLM collector for the checkpoint's DFlash drafter; DFlash arms follow once that curve is committed.\n- **Weights are not pre-staged on `b200-nscale`.** `runners.yaml` has no entry for this model, so `hf:XiaomiMiMo/MiMo-V2.6-Flash-RL` is served from the shared HF hub cache, the same way as DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash on this cluster. The repo is not gated. The first job downloads the checkpoint.\n- **Checkpoint choice.** Xiaomi has since released `XiaomiMiMo/MiMo-V2.6-Flash-MOPD` (same architecture and format), which fixes the RL release's tool-call repetition. This PR stays on the RL checkpoint; MOPD can be added separately.\n- **Not in the engine-mapping table.** This PR adds only the MODELS.md support-matrix row; maintainers decide the model-to-engine mapping.\n- **Not yet run end to end on GPUs.** The first canary failed at server startup on `language-model-only` (fixed above); the `full-sweep-fail-fast` sweep reruns on the latest commit.\n\n<details>\n<summary>AI model disclosure</summary>\n\n- Model/version: Claude Opus 5.5 (`claude-opus-5-5`), via Claude Code. No delegated agents.\n- Role: research, recipe and config authoring, local validation, sweep debugging, and PR preparation.\n\n</details>\n\n<details>\n<summary>PR checklist and change type</summary>\n\n## Type of Change\n\n- [ ] Bug fix\n- [x] New feature\n- [x] Configuration change\n- [x] Documentation update\n- [ ] Other (please describe)\n\n## Checklist\n\n- [x] I have completed the AI model disclosure and kept it current\n- [x] I have tested my changes locally\n- [x] I have updated documentation if necessary\n- [x] **If `python -m infx.matrix.changed --base origin/main` lists affected config k", "body_excerpted": true, "metadata_excerpt": "{\"number\": 4330, \"labels\": [{\"id\": 11216995506, \"node_id\": \"LA_kwDOPLfvps8AAAACnJXEsg\", \"url\": \"https://api.github.com/repos/SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX/labels/full-sweep-fail-fast\", \"name\": \"full-sweep-fail-fast\", \"color\": \"39FB34\", \"default\": false, \"description\": \"Full sweep with canary gate; null}", "metadata_excerpted": false}, {"id": "github:SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX:3427", "source": "github", "kind": "pr", "url": "https://github.com/SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX/pull/3427", "title": "refactor(collectivex): use Python orchestration and consolidate modules / \u7edf\u4e00 Python \u7f16\u6392\u5e76\u5408\u5e76 CollectiveX \u96f6\u6563\u6a21\u5757", "created_at": "2026-09-25T05:18:50Z", "updated_at": "2026-10-18T08:44:00Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-20T12:14:15Z", "state": "closed", "merged_at": null, "parent": null, "content_hash": "15f65da5db4be75c8b329e02dc4a9aa63065effbf5f0493d65936323e2c3e4a4", "body": "## Description\n\nCollectiveX's workflow now calls Python directly. Python owns allocation, image/source staging, backend preparation, rank startup, retries, artifact collection, and cleanup; **all seven original CollectiveX Bash files are removed**. Job steps execute through the real `simple-slurm==0.3.6` API. Explicit `salloc --no-shell`, targeted queue queries, and cancellation preserve the existing allocation model.\n\nThe benchmark side separates the former monolithic harness (1,662 lines at the initial baseline; 1,774 in current main) into orchestration, measurement, correctness, and results. DeepEP/UCCL share compatible Buffer operations. Small helper modules were folded back into their owners: implementation files fell from **41 in the first refactor to 28**, versus **25 at the original baseline**. There is no shell-to-Python argument protocol or generated shell environment file.\n\nThis is intended to preserve behavior: matrix coverage, CLI arguments, workload/timing profiles, event ordering, source/image pins, cache identities, per-pool placement and retries, result schema, failures, and cleanup. All work stays in the worktree created from `origin/main` at `960ff1ffa51e160df422394e0477a4410fa65888`. The final integration merges current main at `4f94801a5` and preserves its concurrently landed CUDA-graph replay and NCCL rank-major/zero-copy changes; those are upstream behavior, not new refactor features.\n\n**The 30% implementation LOC reduction target is not met.** Python plus Bash grows **194 physical lines (+2.1%)**. The final result has fewer files than the first refactor and no repository-owned Bash orchestration, but Python's explicit resource handling, argument lists, and process boundaries do not produce a net line reduction. Tests, error handling, and measurement explanations were not sacrificed to meet a percentage.\n\n### Structure and call flow\n\n`collectivex-sweep.yml \u2192 ci.py \u2192 runtime/execution.py \u2192 simple-slurm srun / Docker torchrun \u2192 runtime/node.py \u2192 bench/run_ep.py \u2192 EPBackend adapter \u2192 ep_harness.run_sweep \u2192 ep_results`\n\n- `ci.py`: matrix/extraction, execution, independent finalization, and private-workspace cleanup.\n- Runtime has **eight substantive modules**, plus its package initializer: `config.py` (pool policies/settings/arguments), `scheduler.py` (Slurm/processes/locks/logs), `probe.py` (hardware/network discovery and selectors), `storage.py` (images and staging), `build.py` (pins/source/build caches), `node.py` (compute-host preparation and rank bootstrap), `execution.py` (shard lifecycle), and `docker.py` (Slurm-less pools).\n- `run_ep.py` owns CLI setup and lazy backend dispatch. `ep_backend.py` owns the abstract transport/input contract. `ep_measurement.py` owns `EPTiming`, event timing, reductions, and samples. `ep_harness.py` orders passes, `ep_oracle.py` checks them, and `ep_results.py` owns identity/accounting/publication.\n- `BufferOperations` in `ep_buffer.py` shares the active DeepEP/UCCL Buffer operations; vendor constructors, quantizers, and different normal transports remain in their adapters. `EPBackend` inherits `EPTiming`; orchestration mostly uses functions and data classes, with `SlurmAllocation` owning one allocation.\n- Swap-blocks uses the same cluster lifecycle with its existing independent benchmark and schema. OperatorX's probe and runtime-directory consumers remain covered.\n\n[English implementation map](https://github.com/SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX/blob/refactor/collectivex-modules/experimental/CollectiveX/docs/architecture.md) \u00b7 [\u4e2d\u6587\u5b9e\u73b0\u5bfc\u89c8](https://github.com/SemiAnalysisAI/InferenceX/blob/refactor/collectivex-modules/experimental/CollectiveX/docs/architecture_zh.md)\n\n### LOC and file accounting\n\nBaseline: current main `4f94801a5`, so upstream changes are excluded from the PR totals. The internal dispatch copy epilogue still runs. This is not borrowed communication-buffer storage or a zero-copy dispatch implementation.\n\n## Regression coverage\n\nThe new `tests/ep/test_recv_lifetime.py` uses the independent NCCL dispatch reference and requires bitwise equality of payloads, local expert indices, routing weights, source-token metadata, and the saved-activation weight gradient.\n\nEach process run covers:\n\n- Deterministic dispatch off/on; output allocation on the compute/communication stream.\n- Uneven inputs, balanced routes, masked routes, and highly skewed routing with empty receivers.\n- Two selected experts on the same destination rank, distinguishing unique receive rows from token/expert assignments.\n- A prefix saved with `ctx.save_for_backward`, followed by three additional dispatches through the same buffer before backward.\n- A delayed reader on another stream, dropping the original receive allocation references and allocating/filling four same-capacity tensors on the allocation stream. The copy destination and allocator warm-up are prepared before the delay. Pressure writes must finish while a **pre-read** event is still pending; a pending post-copy event alone is insufficient.\n\nScheduling can miss the pressure window. All ranks agree on numerical correctness and window validity, then retry in collective lockstep with a longer delay (at most three attempts). Actual corruption fails immediately; an inconclusive window is never silently reported as coverage.\n\nThe consumer handoff uses a CUDA event captured **after the first dispatch-event wait**, including its deterministic-sort hook. It does not depend on repeated waits on the original `EventOverlap`; that is separate from this lifetime coverage.\n\n## Validation\n\n### Current polished revision\n\nHead: `97fa6d18ff717df99acbdbb862325bda3d4a0d46`, based on public main `b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6`.\n\nThe current 166-line test passed on H200 with PyTorch `2.13.0+cu132`, CUDA 13.2 and NCCL 2.32.3. Each native lifetime policy was run once with eight ranks: 24 distributed saved-prefix/backward cases in total, with 24 valid delayed-consumer allocator-pressure windows. This is positive coverage of the current reorganized test; the historical negative and scheduling controls below were not rerun against this revision.\n\n```bash\nEP_AVOID_RECORD_STREAM=0 python tests/ep/test_recv_lifetime.py --num-processes 8 --num-tokens 64 --hidden 512\nEP_AVOID_RECORD_STREAM=1 python tests/ep/test_recv_lifetime.py --num-processes 8 --num-tokens 64 --hidden 512\n```\n\n### Historical validation of the earlier test version\n\nTested commit: `01c31d0d75f155ca00ba933a6b9ab7a01459169e`, based on upstream `93eb6eb238127e96c6d7a4a625a6dad158348509`. The unchanged upstream native extension and the tested source archive/test-file hashes were verified. Today, we are excited to introduce\nDeepEP V2\n, which includes a complete refactoring of Expert Parallelism \u2014 achieving extreme performance with several times fewer SM resources compared to V1, while supporting significantly larger scale-up and scale-out domains \u2014 as well as experimental 0 SM Engram, 0 SM Pipeline Parallelism, and 0 SM Context Parallelism all-gather.\nWe are also happy to announce that we have switched from the NVSHMEM backend to the more lightweight\nNCCL Gin backend\n.\nNew Features\nFully JIT (Just-In-Time compilation)\nNCCL Gin backend\nHeader-only & lightweight\nAble to reuse existing NCCL communicators\nEPv2\nCUDA graph compatible, or faster CPU sync in saving memory mode\nHigh-throughput and low-latency APIs unified into a single interface, with a new GEMM layout\nLarger scale-up & scale-out domain support (up to EP2048)\nAnalytical SM & QP count calculation \u2014 no more auto-tuning needed\nBoth hybrid & direct modes remain supported\nFor V3-like legacy training, SM usage reduced from 24 to 4 - 6 while maintaining equivalent or better performance\n0 SM Engram (with RDMA)\n0 SM PP (with RDMA)\n0 SM CP (with Copy Engine)\nNotes\nBuffer size consumption is larger than V1\n0 SM RDMA low-latency EP is no longer supported\nEngram, PP, and CP are experimental features\nStill On-going Features\nElastic GPU & CPU buffers\n: A contiguous virtual address space that maps to a hybrid of GPU and CPU physical memory under the hood, enabling fully automatic and transparent Engram or imbalanced EP\nReducing intermediate buffer sizes by leveraging EP replay to handle load imbalance\nAll-gather updates and reduce-scatter implementations for DP & TP\nPerformance\nFollowing V3's configuration, we tested with 8K tokens per batch, 7168 hidden dimensions, top 8 experts, FP8 dispatching, and BF16 combining, and obtained the following results:\nArch\nNIC type\nTopo\nDispatch Bottleneck Bandwidth\nCombine Bottleneck Bandwidth\n#SMs\nSM90\nCX7\nEP 8 x 2\n90 GB/s (RDMA)\n81 GB/s (RDMA)\n12\nSM90\nCX7\nEP 8 x 4\n61 GB/s (RDMA)\n61 GB/s (RDMA)\n6\nSM100\nCX7\nEP 8 x 2\n90 GB/s (RDMA)\n91 GB/s (RDMA)\n12\nSM100\nN/A\nEP 8\n726 GB/s (NVLink)\n740 GB/s (NVLink)\n64 (Max perf)\nSM100\nN/A\nEP 8\n643 GB/s (NVLink)\n675 GB/s (NVLink)\n24 (Min #SM)\nNotes, the results are logical bandwidth. For example, under the\nEP 8 x 2\ncase, 90 GB/s actually contains local rank traffic.\nComparing with V1, V2 achieves up to 1.3x peak performance, while saving up to 4x SM count.\nWe omit results for larger EP configurations for the time being, but encourage interested users to benchmark them directly. When a store tile extends past an expert's capacity, it can overwrite valid rows of the next expert. This affects both BF16 and FP8: capacity 32 reproduces it, and capacity 96 also fails when experts are nearly full.\n\nKeep the expert dimension in the output descriptor and reuse the existing 3D TMA store path. This preserves tile selection and the compute pipeline without adding buffers or synchronization. Boundary regressions are integrated into the existing BF16 and FP8 test suites.\n\nThis revisits the limitation discussed in #15 and the small-capacity failure reported in #268. #15 previously fixed small capacities by shrinking the store tile and rejected larger capacities not divisible by the tile size; it also suggested 3D TMA but deferred support for those cases. This change handles both small and non-tile-aligned capacities through the same output path.\n\nOn H200, the median paired latency changes across six rounds were between \u22120.17% and +0.16% for the larger tested shapes. The smallest shape was about 1.9% slower for BF16 and 1.5% slower for FP8, roughly 0.06\u20130.08 \u00b5s. The uniform 3D path avoids maintaining separate aligned and unaligned implementations, at that small measured cost.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Reproduction and validation</summary>\n\nRequires an SM90 GPU and a source build of DeepGEMM. Save the following as `repro.py` outside the source checkout and run `python repro.py` against the installed baseline or patched package:\n\n```python\nimport torch\nimport deep_gemm\nfrom deep_gemm.testing import calc_diff\n\ntorch.manual_seed(29)\na = torch.randn((8, 32, 256), device='cuda', dtype=torch.bfloat16)\nb = torch.randn((8, 256, 256), device='cuda', dtype=torch.bfloat16)\nd = torch.empty((8, 32, 256), device='cuda', dtype=torch.bfloat16)\ncounts = torch.tensor([0, 1, 16, 31, 32, 1, 32, 32],\n                      device='cuda', dtype=torch.int)\nvalid = torch.arange(32, device='cuda')[None, :] < counts[:, None]\nreference = torch.bmm(a.double(), b.double().mT).bfloat16()\n\nfor _ in range(16):\n    deep_gemm.m_grouped_bf16_gemm_nt_masked(a, b, d, counts, 32)\n    diff = calc_diff(d[valid], reference[valid])\n    assert diff < 1e-5, f'{diff=}'\n```\n\nRunning this exact script against the baseline failed with `AssertionError: diff=tensor(0.1456, device='cuda:0', dtype=torch.float64)`; the patched wheel completed all 16 checks. Only valid rows are checked; padding values are unspecified.\n\nRun the included regressions from the checkout after building and installing the patched package:\n\n```bash\ncd tests\npython - <<'PY'\nimport torch\nimport test_bf16\nimport test_fp8_fp4\n\ntorch.manual_seed(17)\ntest_bf16.test_m_grouped_gemm_masked_boundaries()\ntest_fp8_fp4.test_m_grouped_gemm_masked_boundaries()\nPY\n```\n\nBoth passed with the final built wheel, including a fresh-JIT-cache run. Both fail at capacity 16 when run against the baseline package. A separate timed run with the JIT cache populated took 0.23 s for the BF16 test body and 0.09 s for FP8; this excludes process startup and imports, and does not estimate cold compilation time.\n\nSupplemental validation, using separate local harnesses not included in this PR:\n\n- Base: `057ca5964aae0879ff2e0eb71ee05a3cb0ba3df7`; candidate: `20865936a058d55c4c583cc2b0c2bcf3bc319a2f`. H200, PyTorch `2.13.0+cu130`, CUDA 13.0.\n- 34 configurations per arm, covering BF16 and FP8, capacities from 1 to 257, single-expert and all-empty cases; 16 eager and 16 Graph calls each. Baseline and revert each had 24 failing configurations; the fix had none. Two configurations were all-empty and only checked completion. The revert reused the original package in a fresh process/cache.\n- Independent FP64 matmul references, rounded to BF16; FP8 references used the actual quantized inputs and scales. No run-to-run changes were observed in the fixed panel.\n- 37 adjacent-path checks passed, covering ordinary GEMM layouts, output dtypes and accumulation, batched GEMM, and K-grouped GEMM.\n- Native `python setup.py bdist_wheel` passed with setuptools `75.8.0`.\n\nPerformance used six paired rounds with alternating arm order. Each measurement took the median of seven CUDA-event samples, each containing 1,000 Graph calls. Input generation and quantization were outside timing; FP8 API scale conversion was included. Positive values below mean slower; values are medians of paired latency changes.\n\n| Groups | Capacity | N | K | Expected M | BF16 | FP8 |\n|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| 8 | 128 | 256 | 256 | 64 | +1.931% | +1.526% |\n| 32 | 4096 | 1536 | 2048 | 20 | +0.070% | \u22120.143% |\n| 6 | 4096 | 4096 | 4096 | 1024 | +0.155% | +0.023% |\n| 32 | 4096 | 6144 | 7168 | 192 | \u22120.167% | \u22120.056% |\n\nClocks were not locked; sampled SM clocks ranged from 1305 to 1980 MHz. Small differences on the larger shapes should not be interpreted as established speedups. CPU launch latency, model-level impact, other GPU architectures, and the full build matrix were not evaluated.\n\n</details", "body_excerpted": true, "metadata_excerpt": "{\"number\": 469, \"labels\": [], \"comments\": 0, \"state_reason\": null}", "metadata_excerpted": false}, {"id": "github:deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM:461", "source": "github", "kind": "issue", "url": "https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM/issues/461", "title": "Update CUTLASS to 4.8.0 for SM107 (Rubin) support", "created_at": "2026-09-28T21:58:56Z", "updated_at": "2026-10-17T13:43:18Z", "fetched_at": "2026-10-20T12:14:23Z", "state": "open", "merged_at": null, "parent": null, "content_hash": "f38bdd8bfaf49de833eeccf7026351804272f4b3bf4105d246d5f83381a2ab3b", "body": "## Summary\n\nWe encountered TMA/TMEM device assertions while running DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash through vLLM on Rubin (SM107). Could DeepGEMM update its vendored CUTLASS dependency from **4.2.1 to 4.8.0**, which adds initial SM107 support?\n\nThe failure was observed with `vllm-project/DeepGEMM` at `e1f418c2a4f20818221f6b0e578b4c2f634d4c3f`. We also confirmed that upstream [`deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM` at `78b6900`](https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM/tree/78b69000794d0937b47ae3387eff7663410264d1/third-party) carries the same CUTLASS pin, `f3fde58372d33e9a5650ba7b80fc48b3b49d40c8`. The dependency limitation is therefore shared, although we have not reproduced the runtime failure on an unmodified upstream checkout.\n\nThe DSV4.1-Flash error output is included below. A colleague reported similar behavior with GLM5.3 on Rubin, which suggests the issue is not model-specific.\n\n## Observed failure\n\nIn our September 22, 2026 run, model weights loaded, but startup profiling failed before requests could be served. With `CUDA_LAUNCH_BLOCKING=1`, the stack pointed to `tf32_hc_prenorm_gemm`:\n\n```text\nTrying to use TMA Descriptor Prefetch without CUTE_ARCH_TMA_SM90_ENABLED.\nAttempting to use TMEM allocation PTX without CUTE_ARCH_TCGEN05_TMEM_ENABLED\nCUDA error 710 (CUDA_ERROR_ASSERT): device-side assert triggered\n```\n\nDeepGEMM's [pinned architecture selector](https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepJIT/blob/e5bdee2bc4ca519eba00cfc5f0c6e950e6a96a16/include/deep_jit/backend/cuda/device.hpp#L68-L79) selects `sm_107f` for capability 10.7. However, the [pinned CUTLASS headers](https://github.com/NVIDIA/cutlass/blob/f3fde58372d33e9a5650ba7b80fc48b3b49d40c8/include/cutlass/arch/config.h) lack SM107 enablement; their SM100 feature guards require `__CUDA_ARCH__ == 1000`. The errors indicate that the affected CuTe TMA/TMEM helpers were compiled without their feature macros enabled, selecting assertion fallbacks. This is consistent with an architecture-guard mismatch.\n\n## Environment\n\n| Component | Recorded value |\n|---|---|\n| GPU | Rubin, SM107 / compute capability 10.7 |\n| Host architecture | Linux ARM64 |\n| Driver | 620.43 |\n| CUDA runtime | 13.4; exact NVCC patch version was not captured |\n| Python | 3.12.3 |\n| PyTorch | `2.15.0.dev20260908+cu134` |\n| vLLM | `0.29.1rc1.dev513+g0961bbae2.d20260922.cu134` |\n| vLLM source | `0961bbae2894d574be790d219651824eb199318e` |\n| DeepGEMM | vLLM fork, `e1f418c2a4f20818221f6b0e578b4c2f634d4c3f` |\n| Current CUTLASS | **4.2.1**, `f3fde58372d33e9a5650ba7b80fc48b3b49d40c8` |\n| Model | `deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash` |\n| Model revision | `dba1be0a40aa45a94ad051997016db3960a90277` |\n\nBase container:\n\n```text\nvllm/vllm-openai:cu134-nightly-0961bbae2894d574be790d219651824eb199318e@sha256:551a8c08c5119b5dbc7e902aabcd6ce2d903f084f76d4fa13fd887008bfd83d3\n```\n\nConfiguration: TP4, V2 model runner, eager execution, CPU-offloaded Engram, no speculative decoding, maximum model length 32768, maximum sequences 8, and maximum batched tokens 8192. The optional `mooncake-transfer-engine-cuda13==0.3.13.post1` package was installed, but the Mooncake connector was disabled for this diagnostic run.\n\n## Proposed update and current workaround\n\nUpdate the CUTLASS submodule pin from **4.2.1 to [4.8.0](https://github.com/NVIDIA/cutlass/releases/tag/v4.8.0)**, released **September 21, 2026 PDT / September 22 UTC**. This release adds initial Rubin support, including enabling existing SM100-compatible kernels for `sm_107a` and `sm_107f` and updating TMEM handling.\n\nThe release notes specify the R615 driver associated with CUDA 13.4 GA for SM107 execution and state that R610 from the Developer Preview is insufficient. Our recorded run used driver **620.43**.\n\nOur temporary workaround sets DeepGEMM's JIT compiler target to `sm_100f` when the device reports capability 10.7. non-deterministic results in sm90 tf32_hc_prenorm_gemm \u00b7 Issue #449 \u00b7 deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM \u00b7 GitHub", "created_at": null, "updated_at": null, "fetched_at": "2026-10-20T12:15:52Z", "state": null, "merged_at": null, "parent": "github:deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM:449", "content_hash": "c32735ab59c6f6bbe35c9a182dad44db1758ef75f18e4e242c43d4d50b619d0c", "body": "[Bug] non-deterministic results in sm90 tf32_hc_prenorm_gemm\n#449\nNew issue\nCopy link\nNew issue\nCopy link\nOpen\nOpen\n[Bug] non-deterministic results in sm90 tf32_hc_prenorm_gemm\n#449\nCopy link\nDescription\nleevan\nopened\non Sep 16, 2026\nIssue body actions\nDescription\nOn SM90 GPUs, tf32_hc_prenorm_gemm can produce incorrect and non-deterministic GEMM outputs when processing multiple K blocks.\nRepeated calls with identical inputs can return different results.\nRoot cause\nThe kernel uses asynchronous WGMMA instructions with register-source A operands. Previously, the next iteration loaded its A fragment\nbefore waiting for the preceding iteration's WGMMA to complete. This could overwrite A registers while they were still being read by\nthe in-flight WGMMA.\nFix\nProposed fix:\n#448\nThe fix moves\nwarpgroup_wait<0>()\nimmediately after\nwarpgroup_commit_batch()\n, ensuring that each iteration's WGMMA completes\nbefore its A registers are reused.\nThe PR also adds a regression test covering split-K, PDL enabled/disabled, and default/side streams. It checks outputs against FP32\nreferences and verifies repeatability across identical launches.\nValidation\nWith the fix applied, both the existing hyperconnection tests and all 24 configurations of the new regression test passed on an SM90\nGPU.\nTODO\nfix will introduce some performance regression which suppose to be mitigated in upcoming PRs\u3002\nReactions are currently unavailable\nActivity\nLiRunGuo\ncommented\non\nSep 22, 2026\nLiRunGuo\non Sep 22, 2026\nMore actions\nI measured this on an H200 (SM90, driver 580.159.03, PyTorch 2.11.0+cu130) with DeepGEMM\nmain\n(\n78b6900\n), in case it helps the review of\n#448\n.\nReproduction.\nI ran the 24 shapes from\ntest_hc_prenorm_gemm\n(\nm\nin 13 / 137 / 4096 / 8192,\nn = 24\n,\nk\nin 7168 / 7680 / 28672, with and without 16 splits), with 50 back-to-back launches per shape. I did this with both NVCC 12.9.86 and 13.0.48.\nmain\ngave 0 bitwise mismatches in every case, and the regression test added in\n#448\nalso passes on\nmain\nwith both compilers (24 / 24).\nI also checked the SASS of\nmain\nfrom both compilers with a small script. It looks for writes to the A registers of an in-flight\nHGMMA\ngroup before the\nWARPGROUP.DEPBAR\nthat waits for it, and found none: ptxas puts the A fragments of consecutive stages in different registers. So the source-level race is real, but these two ptxas versions do not turn it into a hazard for these instantiations. That may explain why it is hard to reproduce, and it could still show up with other compiler versions.\nPerformance of\n#448\n.\nMedian of 3\nbench_kineto\nruns, NVCC 12.9, in \u00b5s:\nm, k, splits\nmain\n#448\ndouble-buffered A\n137, 28672, none\n106.5\n120.8 (+13%)\n111.4 (+5%)\n4096, 7680, none\n32.9\n36.2 (+10%)\n33.8 (+3%)\n4096, 7680, 16\n45.5\n38.0 (\u221216%)\n47.6 (+5%)\n8192, 28672, none\n123.5\n131.8 (+7%)\n125.2 (+1%)\nThe geometric mean over all 24 shapes versus\nmain\nis +2.8% for\n#448\nand +2.2% for the double-buffered variant (NVCC 13.0: +4.6% and +2.9%).\n#448\ncosts 8\u201313% on most shapes without splits, but it is about 16% faster on the\nk = 7680\n, 16-split shapes.\nThe double-buffered variant keeps two A register buffers selected by stage parity, and waits with one WGMMA group in flight (\nwarpgroup_wait<1>\n) before a buffer is reused. It fixes the race by construction and recovers most of the loss on the shapes without splits, but it does not get\n#448\n's gain on the 16-split shapes. So it is not clearly better overall. The patch is on\nLiRunGuo/DeepGEMM@sm90-hc-prenorm-double-buffered-a\nif it is useful.\nleevan\ncommented\non\nSep 23, 2026\nleevan\non Sep 23, 2026\nAuthor\nMore actions\n@LiRunGuo\ncool, maybe you can try with a H20. regarding the performance, I do have another version of double buffer which improves the performance.\nLiRunGuo\ncommented\non\nSep 29, 2026\nLiRunGuo\non Sep 29, 2026\nMore actions\n@leevan\nThanks for the pointer. On transformers 5.x, the RoPE config lives in\nrope_parameters\n, so the extension is merged in there (keeping the base rope_theta) rather than set on the old\nrope_scaling\nattribute. It uses a real Qwen3-8B config if the Hub is reachable, otherwise a synthetic one.\nWhat I did not run is a full long-context training pass, which also needs the target cache regenerated at the long context. reload this page\n.\nCopy link\nCopy Markdown\nSummary\nFile\nBug\nFix\nImpact\ndeepspec/utils/sampling.py\nDraft samples were drawn from native-dtype probabilities while rejection used float32 probabilities\nCast logits to float32 before temperature scaling and softmax in\nsample_tokens\nRemoves a speculative-decoding distribution mismatch\ndeepspec/utils/optim.py\nWarmup wrapper delegated to\nCosineAnnealingLR.get_lr()\nin a bad handoff state\nReturn the configured peak/base LRs directly at the handoff\nPrevents LR overshoot above the configured peak\ndeepspec/modeling/eagle3/common.py\nEagle3 fallback 4D mask used a key dimension shorter than\nkv_len\nonce cache was present\nExtend the 2D keep-mask to\nkv_len\nbefore building the additive mask\nFixes the non-flex cached attention path and preserves padded cache columns\ndeepspec/modeling/dspark/loss.py\nDistributed\ntrain/loss\nmetric was rank-weighted instead of token/objective-weighted\nLog the globally scaled\nbackward_loss\nwith\ndp_mean\nMakes logged DSpark loss match the global training objective\nSuggested review order: sampling, scheduler, Eagle3 mask, then DSpark metric logging. Each change is isolated in its own commit.\n1. Draft sampling dtype mismatch\nlogits_to_probs\ncomputes the draft distribution as:\np\n=\nsoftmax\n(\nlogits\n.\nfloat\n()\n/\nT\n)\nand uses that float32\np\nin the speculative acceptance ratio\nmin(1, q / p)\nand correction distribution. Before this PR,\nsample_tokens\nsampled through the native-dtype path instead, so bfloat16 logits produced a bfloat16 probability tensor passed to\ntorch.multinomial\n. In effect, the draft token was drawn from a represented distribution\nr\n, but acceptance and correction were computed as if it came from\np\n.\nFor a one-token speculative step, with sample distribution\nr\nbut acceptance math using\np\n:\na_y = min(1, q_y / p_y)\nc_y = (q_y - p_y)_+ / sum_z (q_z - p_z)_+\nPr[out = y] = r_y a_y + (sum_x r_x (1 - a_x)) c_y\nWhen\nr = p\n, this collapses to the target distribution\nq\n. When\nr != p\n, it generally does not.\nA minimal non-degenerate example demonstrates the final marginal bias:\np = [0.3, 0.7]\nr = [0.30078125, 0.69921875]\nq = [0.4, 0.6]\n\na = [1, 0.6 / 0.7]\nc = [1, 0]\n\nPr[out = 0] = 0.30078125 + 0.69921875 * (1 - 0.6 / 0.7)\n            = 0.40066964\nq[0]        = 0.40000000\nThe exact bf16 error bound is not the important part of the fix, but the scale is enough to matter: bf16 has 7 explicit mantissa bits, spacing near 1 of\n2^-7 ~= 0.78%\n, and round-to-nearest error on the order of\n2^-8 ~= 0.39%\nfor normal-range values.\nThis PR changes\nsample_tokens\nto cast before temperature scaling and softmax:\nflat_logits\n=\nlogits\n.\nreshape\n(\n-\n1\n,\nvocab_size\n).\nfloat\n()\n/\ntemperature\nThat makes the sampled draft distribution match the\np\nused by rejection sampling.\n2. Warmup to cosine LR overshoot\nThis is a wrapper state-transition bug, not ordinary\nCosineAnnealingLR\nbehavior.\nAt the warmup/cosine handoff, the old wrapper delegated to the cosine scheduler's chainable\nget_lr()\npath with:\ncosine.last_epoch == 0\ncosine._is_initial == False\noptimizer.lr == warmup_peak\nThat is not the ordinary closed-form epoch-0 cosine value. The recursive update applies:\nLR = eta_min + 2 / (1 + cos(pi / T_max)) * (LR_current - eta_min)\nWith\neta_min = 0\nand\nLR_current = peak_lr\n, the transition becomes:\nLR_transition = 2 / (1 + cos(pi / T_max)) * peak_lr\nSince\ncos(pi / T_max) < 1\nfor\nT_max > 1\n, this is above the configured peak. Examples:\nT_max\nfactor\novershoot\n5\n1.10557\n+10.56%\n10\n1.02509\n+2.51%\n50\n1.00099\n+0.0988%\n100\n1.00025\n+0.0247%\nThis PR special-cases the handoff to return the configured peak/base LRs directly instead of calling the recursive\nget_lr()\nbranch at\nlast_epoch == 0\n. The following scheduler step then advances cosine normally from epoch 0 to epoch 1.\n3. Eagle3 fallback mask crash\nprepare_4d_causal_attention_mask\nneeds the additive padding-mask key dimension to equal\nkv_len\n.\nAt TTT step\nk >= 1\n:\nq_len = seq_len\npast_seen_tokens = k * seq_len\nkv_len = past_seen_tokens + seq_len = (k + 1) * seq_len\nThe causal mask has shape\n[1, 1, q_len, kv_len]\n, but the incoming 2D\nattention_mask\ncovers one fixed-size chunk, so it has shape\n[B, seq_len]\n. Expanding or slicing it cannot create cached-key columns:\ncausal:        [1, 1, seq_len, (k + 1) * seq_len]\nexpanded_mask: [B, 1, 1, seq_len]\nAdding those tensors fails on dim 3 for every cached step where\nkv_len != seq_len\n. For\nseq_len = 4, k = 1\n, this is\n[1,1,4,8] + [B,1,1,4]\n.\nThe initial fix padded the missing columns as valid, but that was too broad: if earlier cached chunks include padded positions, it would incorrectly make those cached padding slots attendable. This PR now extends the keep-mask by repeating the fixed chunk mask pattern until it reaches\nkv_len\n, then slices to\nkv_len\n. That fixes the shape invariant while preserving padded columns in cached chunks. actions\nSummary and request for model-team triage\nWe observe DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash entering native-token reasoning loops during long, tool-enabled conversations. It repeatedly announces that it will emit an answer or tool call, but produces neither. Some outputs run to the token limit; others are stopped by our resource-protection observer.\nWe are asking for the canonical V4.1 prompt/history contract and help routing this report to the model/inference team. The official model card recommends deepseek-recipe and invites issues or contact at\nservice@deepseek.com\n. Our failed runs used vLLM's V4.1 encoder, not deepseek-recipe itself; this is\nnot a claim that recipe caused the loops\n. Please redirect us if another official tracker is preferred.\nEnvironment and workload\nModel: DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash open weights. This is a local-inference report, not a demonstrated failure of the official hosted API.\nEngine in the reported Spark experiments: vLLM 0.28.1rc1.dev388+g8a728663c.\nHardware: four NVIDIA DGX Spark / GB10 nodes forming one TP4/DCP4 model service.\nRecorded precision: BF16 engine dtype, MXFP4 routed-expert weights, fp8_ds_mla main attention KV. These refer to different objects.\nMain fixed replay input: 107 messages, 26 tool definitions, 74,657 actual input tokens.\nSampling in the completed five-arm experiment: thinking enabled, numeric reasoning_effort=100, temperature=1.0, top_p=0.95, min_p=0, top_k=-1, max_tokens=8192, global concurrency 4.\nDSpark enabled in that experiment: num_speculative_tokens=5, probabilistic draft sampling, block rejection sampling, adaptive verification disabled.\nA thinking-budget sampler fix was present in the budget experiment; those results are implementation-specific mitigation evidence, not an unmodified-vLLM or official parameter recommendation.\nExpected vs. actual behavior\nExpected: complete the reasoning phase and emit valid tool calls or a final answer.\nActual native output contains repeated procedural phrases, for example:\nWriting.\nLet me output.\nGo.\nOK.\nWriting.\nLet me write.\nGo.\nNow.\nFor baseline seed 21020, the saved output contains 2,158 native generated tokens before observer cancellation, 8,262 reasoning characters, empty content, and no tool calls. An exact 20-token period starts at generated position 1,987 (one-based) and repeats for at least eight cycles. We rechecked equality directly against the saved token IDs:\n[\n339\n,\n46837\n,\n339\n,\n5718\n,\n678\n,\n5238\n,\n339\n,\n5188\n,\n339\n,\n11932\n,\n339\n,\n46837\n,\n339\n,\n5718\n,\n678\n,\n5085\n,\n339\n,\n5188\n,\n339\n,\n8197\n]\nThe five confirmed baseline loops were independently rechecked:\nSeed\nExact-cycle onset, generated token position (1-based)\nPeriod, tokens\nConsecutive cycles verified\n21020\n1987\n20\n8\n21044\n2418\n15\n10\n21054\n2308\n12\n10\n21068\n2916\n15\n8\n21098\n3438\n13\n9\nThese are engine-token loops within one generation. Client request repetition is not needed to produce these captured cycles.\nControlled observations\n1. Fixed-input, five-arm experiment\nEach arm dispatched exactly 100 requests; sent failures were not replaced.\nArm, single parameter change\nComplete non-repeat responses\nConfirmed repeats\nService/transport unknowns\nA: no thinking budget or penalty\n88\n5\n7\nB: thinking budget 1536\n92\n0\n8\nC: frequency_penalty=0.2\n88\n0\n12\nD: presence_penalty=0.2\n88\n5\n7\nE: repetition_penalty=1.05\n92\n2\n6\nUnknowns are not clean negatives. A complete response does not prove business-task success. A separate 100-task quality evaluation per arm scored A/B/C/D/E as 91/93/84/91/92 passes; service failures remain in the denominators, so these numbers do not establish quality-neutral mitigation.\n2. Identical input IDs, different generation trajectories\nWe compared one normal baseline seed (21000), the five looping baseline seeds above, and their six budget-arm counterparts. All 12 captured prompt-token arrays were identical, length 74,657.\nFor the five looping baseline seeds, the corresponding budget-arm responses completed with tool calls. Their reasoning lengths were 1536, 175, 341, 299, and 286 tokens respectively. Only one reached the budget. The four early completions cannot all be explained by forcibly cutting off an existing loop at 1536. We have not established why these trajectories diverge before budget exhaustion.\n3. DSpark / CUDA-graph pilot\nA separate 16-request, effort50 pilot yielded phrase-threshold repeat cancellations:\nDSpark\nGraph mode\nRepeat cancellations / attempts\nenabled\nFULL_AND_PIECEWISE\n2/4\ndisabled\nNONE\n2/4\ndisabled\nFULL_AND_PIECEWISE\n1/4\nenabled\nNONE\n0/4\nGraph NONE here is not a claim that enforce-eager was used. These refer to different objects.\n- Main fixed replay input: 107 messages, 26 tool definitions, 74,657 actual input tokens.\n- Sampling in the completed five-arm experiment: thinking enabled, numeric reasoning_effort=100, temperature=1.0, top_p=0.95, min_p=0, top_k=-1, max_tokens=8192, global concurrency 4.\n- DSpark enabled in that experiment: num_speculative_tokens=5, probabilistic draft sampling, block rejection sampling, adaptive verification disabled.\n- A thinking-budget sampler fix was present in the budget experiment; those results are implementation-specific mitigation evidence, not an unmodified-vLLM or official parameter recommendation.\n\n## Expected vs. actual behavior\n\nExpected: complete the reasoning phase and emit valid tool calls or a final answer.\n\nActual native output contains repeated procedural phrases, for example:\n\n```text\nWriting.\nLet me output.\nGo.\nOK.\nWriting.\nLet me write.\nGo.\nNow.\n```\n\nFor baseline seed 21020, the saved output contains 2,158 native generated tokens before observer cancellation, 8,262 reasoning characters, empty content, and no tool calls. An exact 20-token period starts at generated position 1,987 (one-based) and repeats for at least eight cycles. We rechecked equality directly against the saved token IDs:\n\n```json\n[339,46837,339,5718,678,5238,339,5188,339,11932,339,46837,339,5718,678,5085,339,5188,339,8197]\n```\n\nThe five confirmed baseline loops were independently rechecked:\n\n| Seed | Exact-cycle onset, generated token position (1-based) | Period, tokens | Consecutive cycles verified |\n|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| 21020 | 1987 | 20 | 8 |\n| 21044 | 2418 | 15 | 10 |\n| 21054 | 2308 | 12 | 10 |\n| 21068 | 2916 | 15 | 8 |\n| 21098 | 3438 | 13 | 9 |\n\nThese are engine-token loops within one generation. Client request repetition is not needed to produce these captured cycles.\n\n## Controlled observations\n\n### 1. Fixed-input, five-arm experiment\n\nEach arm dispatched exactly 100 requests; sent failures were not replaced.\n\n| Arm, single parameter change | Complete non-repeat responses | Confirmed repeats | Service/transport unknowns |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| A: no thinking budget or penalty | 88 | 5 | 7 |\n| B: thinking budget 1536 | 92 | 0 | 8 |\n| C: frequency_penalty=0.2 | 88 | 0 | 12 |\n| D: presence_penalty=0.2 | 88 | 5 | 7 |\n| E: repetition_penalty=1.05 | 92 | 2 | 6 |\n\nUnknowns are not clean negatives. A complete response does not prove business-task success. A separate 100-task quality evaluation per arm scored A/B/C/D/E as 91/93/84/91/92 passes; service failures remain in the denominators, so these numbers do not establish quality-neutral mitigation.\n\n### 2. Identical input IDs, different generation trajectories\n\nWe compared one normal baseline seed (21000), the five looping baseline seeds above, and their six budget-arm counterparts. All 12 captured prompt-token arrays were identical, length 74,657.\n\nFor the five looping baseline seeds, the corresponding budget-arm responses completed with tool calls. Their reasoning lengths were 1536, 175, 341, 299, and 286 tokens respectively. Only one reached the budget. The four early completions cannot all be explained by forcibly cutting off an existing loop at 1536. We have not established why these trajectories diverge before budget exhaustion.\n\n### 3. DSpark / CUDA-graph pilot\n\nA separate 16-request, effort50 pilot yielded phrase-threshold repeat cancellations:\n\n| DSpark | Graph mode | Repeat cancellations / attempts |\n|---|---|---:|\n| enabled | FULL_AND_PIECEWISE | 2/4 |\n| disabled | NONE | 2/4 |\n| disabled | FULL_AND_PIECEWISE | 1/4 |\n| enabled | NONE | 0/4 |\n\nGraph NONE here is not a claim that enforce-eager was used. This small pilot cannot estimate production incidence, but neither DSpark nor graph execution is a necessary condition for the observed loops.\n\n### 4. This can be solved by validated cuBLAS/cuBLASLt coverage; a new FlashInfer kernel is only needed where the library path is missing or underperforms.\n\nThe GB200 profile attributes roughly 2.27 seconds of summed GPU kernel time to dense projection GEMMs and 0.30 seconds to MLA batched GEMMs over two optimizer steps on one rank. The largest projection is `o_proj`; smaller output dimensions and the FP32 router projection also need attention.\n\n## Requested outcome\n\nCover BF16 forward, input-gradient (`dgrad`), and weight-gradient (`wgrad`) GEMMs with FP32 accumulation. Accept the operand layouts actually used by training, including transposed weight views, without forcing additional copies.\n\nFor activations `X[T,K]`, weights `W[N,K]`, and output gradient `G[T,N]`, support the following operations. The reference layouts below assume contiguous row-major `X`, `W`, and `G`; all strides are in elements, not bytes.\n\n| Operation | Usage / computation | Left operand: shape; strides | Right operand: shape; strides | Requested output: shape; strides |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Forward | `F.linear(X, W)` = `X @ W.T` | `[T,K]`; `(K,1)` | `[K,N]`; `(1,K)` | `[T,N]`; `(N,1)` |\n| Input gradient | `G @ W` | `[T,N]`; `(N,1)` | `[N,K]`; `(K,1)` | `[T,K]`; `(K,1)` |\n| Weight gradient | `G.T @ X` | `[N,T]`; `(1,N)` | `[T,K]`; `(K,1)` | `[N,K]`; `(K,1)` |\n\nThe transposes in this table are views, not requests to materialize transposed copies. Use explicit strides rather than relying only on `NN`/`NT`/`TN` labels, whose interpretation depends on the API's storage convention.\n\nProvide BF16 output and an FP32 gradient-output option where supported. The router's FP32 arithmetic is a separate numerical requirement: do not silently replace it with BF16 or TF32; any faster reduced-precision mode must be explicit and validated.\n\n## Workload and reference shapes\n\n**`T` is irregular, generally not a power of two, and varies across batches and runs.** Recorded values are 16,231 and 16,172, not a fixed 16,384-token batch. Forward and dgrad have ragged row counts; wgrad has a ragged reduction length, and all three must support these sizes without caller-side padding.\n\nThe following table gives logical forward dimensions for the `F.linear(X, W)` pattern above, with weights stored as `[N,K]`. The backward shapes and representative layouts follow from the operation table.\n\n| Projection | `K` | `N` | Input dtype |\n|---|---:|---:|---|\n| Attention output (`o_proj`) | 16,384 | 6,144 | BF16 |\n| Query low-rank projection (`q_a`) | 6,144 | 2,048 | BF16 |\n| Query expansion (`q_b`) | 2,048 | 16,384 | BF16 |\n| KV projection (`kv_a`) | 6,144 | 576 | BF16 |\n| Shared-expert gate/up, each | 6,144 | 2,048 | BF16 |\n| Shared-expert down | 2,048 | 6,144 | BF16 |\n| Dense MLP gate/up, each | 6,144 | 12,288 | BF16 |\n| Dense MLP down | 12,288 | 6,144 | BF16 |\n| Indexer query projection | 2,048 | 4,096 | BF16 |\n| Indexer key projection | 6,144 | 128 | BF16 |\n| Indexer head-weight projection | 6,144 | 32 | BF16 |\n| Router gate | 6,144 | 256 | FP32 |\n\nThe LM-head projection `[T,6144] @ [6144,154880]` is covered by the separate chunked LM-head/loss request. Its GEMMs may reuse this implementation, but its loss and workspace requirements belong to that request.\n\n### MLA batched projections: usage and layout\n\nFor these contractions, heads are the GEMM batch dimension, but activations use token-major `[T,H,D]` storage. A logical `[H,T,D]` batched-GEMM view must therefore not be assumed to have contiguous head-major storage.\n\nThe two forward contractions are shown below. The `einsum` expressions define the math; the output-layout requirements are stated separately because `einsum` alone does not specify physical output strides.\n\n| Operation | Usage / contraction | Logical inputs | Required output interface |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Query absorption | `einsum(\"thd,hdc->thc\", Q, W_uk)` | `Q[T,64,192]`, `W_uk[64,192,512]` | Contiguous `[T,64,512]` for sparse attention |\n| Value projection after attention | `einsum(\"thc,hdc->thd\", A, W_uv)` | `A[T,64,512]`, `W_uv[64,256,512]` | Contiguous `[T,64,256]`, viewable as `[T,16384]` for `o_proj` |\n\nThe query and weights are slices of larger tensors, not compact independent allocations. These representative layouts follow from their parent buffers:\n\n```python\n# Parent buffers are contiguous in the stated order.\nQ = q_full[:, :, :192]       # q_full: [T,64,256]\nW_uk = W_kv[:, :192, :]      # W_kv:   [64,448,512]\nW_uv = W_kv[:, 192:, :]\n\n# Current query-absorption call: write directly into token-major output.\nq_latent = empty((T,64,512))\nbmm(Q.permute(1,0,2), W_uk, out=q_latent.permute(1,0,2))\n\n# Value projection is followed immediately by flattening for o_proj.\nout = einsum(\"thc,hdc->thd\", A, W_uv)\nout = out.reshape(T, 64 * 256)\n```\n\nIn this example, `A` is the token-major attention output `[T,64,512]`. "4e8cccde48253a0d309db1c05f52674b03d9dbfef838911924fd6ea03f97a2c0", "body": "<!-- .github/pull_request_template.md -->\n\n## \ud83d\udccc Description\n\nAdds the seeded reject-or-correct fuzzer for `BatchMLAPagedAttentionWrapper` scoped in #3605. The independent FP32 oracle reconstructs logical requests from their original page mapping and checks output and LSE without reusing wrapper metadata lowering.\n\nThe fuzzer covers ragged requests, exact page boundaries, CSR/dense metadata, packed/split inputs, BF16/FP16, per-backend deterministic output, output and padding poison, plan/run mutations, and CUDA graph replay/replan. It defaults to 150 seeds and supports `FLASHINFER_MLA_FUZZ_SEED`, `FLASHINFER_MLA_FUZZ_NUM_TESTS`, and `FLASHINFER_MLA_FUZZ_ONLY_SEED`.\n\nIt also fixes an exposed FA dispatch gap: direct `run_from_wrapper` now reuses the planned-input validation already used by tuning. A shortened query with automatically allocated output, mismatched head/page dimensions, or insufficient KV pool is rejected before native dispatch. Existing mock wrapper fixtures now use aligned 8/8-dimensional tensors and a pool that actually contains their planned pages 7 and 8.\n\n**Draft for oracle and contract review. The test matrix is not green; unresolved findings are listed below.**\n\n## \ud83d\udd0d Related Issues\n\n- Refs #3605, specifically the [MLA slice](https://github.com/flashinfer-ai/flashinfer/issues/3605#issuecomment-5590855985).\n- #6741 tracks missing CUTLASS KV page-pool validation. No Hopper/FA3 computation has been validated yet.\n\n| Scope | Result |\n| --- | --- |\n| Fuzzer coverage: full run plus targeted replacement runs for changed mutation/graph cases | 544 passed, 238 hardware-skipped, 6 quarantined xfails, **171 failed**; 959 unique cases |\n| Existing wrapper regression subset | 109 passed |\n| CPU sensitivity tests: drop first page / last token / last 1/8 chunk, FP16/BF16, lengths 128/4096 | 12 passed (included in the fuzzer total) |\n| Remote Ruff check and format check; diff whitespace check | Passed |\n\nThe 171 remaining failures are **test cases, not distinct confirmed bugs**:\n- 121 non-finite-output cases: 119 explicit CUTLASS cases and 2 `auto` cases, with NaN in unused KV padding.\n- 50 cases exceed the tightened numerical thresholds: 20 FA2, 20 `auto`, and 10 CUTLASS.
